**Apple Inc.** is an American multinational technology company headquartered in [Cupertino, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cupertino,_California), in [Silicon Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_Valley), and known for consumer electronics, software and online services. Founded in 1976 as Apple Computer Company by [Steve Jobs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Jobs), [Steve Wozniak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Wozniak) and [Ronald Wayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Wayne), the company was incorporated by Jobs and Wozniak as Apple Computer, Inc. the following year. Its current name was adopted in 2007 as the company expanded its focus from computers to consumer electronics. Apple is one of the [Big Tech](57174306.md) companies.

The company was founded to market Wozniak's [Apple I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_I) computer. Its successor, the [Apple II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_II_\(original\)), became one of the first successful mass-produced personal [microcomputers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microcomputer). Apple introduced the [Lisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Lisa) in 1983 and the [Macintosh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh_128K) in 1984, popularizing [graphical user interfaces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graphical_user_interface) navigated by a [mouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_mouse). By 1985, internal conflicts led to Jobs leaving the company to form [NeXT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NeXT) and Wozniak stepping back from active employment. During the 1990s, Apple lost considerable market share to lower-priced [Wintel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wintel) computers, [Intel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intel)-powered [PC clones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PC_clones) running [Windows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windows). By 1997, Apple was reporting major losses and struggling to deliver a modernized operating system, leading it to acquire NeXT, which brought Jobs back to the company. Under his leadership, Apple returned to profitability through the [iMac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMac), [iPod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPod), [iPhone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone), and [iPad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPad), the [iTunes Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITunes_Store), the [Apple Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Store) retail chain, and the "[Think different](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Think_different)" advertising campaign. Jobs resigned in 2011 for health reasons and died later that year.

[Apple's product lineup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Apple_products) includes portable and home hardware like the iPhone, iPad, [Mac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mac_\(computer\)), [Apple Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Watch), and [AirPods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AirPods); [several in-house operating systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Apple_operating_systems) such as [iOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IOS), [iPadOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPadOS), and [macOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacOS); and [various software](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_software_by_Apple) and services including [Apple Pay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Pay) and [iCloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICloud), as well as multimedia streaming services like [Apple Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Music) and [Apple TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_TV_\(streaming_service\)). Since 2011, Apple has for the most part been the world's [largest company by market capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_public_corporations_by_market_capitalization), and, as of 2024, is the [largest manufacturing company by revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_manufacturing_companies_by_revenue), the [fourth-largest PC vendor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_share_of_personal_computer_vendors#Current_top_vendors_market_share_\(2023\)), the [largest vendor of tablet computers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tablet_computer#By_manufacturer), and the [largest vendor of mobile phones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_mobile_phones#2024). Apple became the first publicly traded US company to be [valued at over \$1 trillion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trillion-dollar_company) in 2018, and, as of October 2025, is valued at just over \$4 trillion.

[Apple has received criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Apple_Inc.) regarding [its contractors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_supply_chain)' labor conditions, [its relationship with trade unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Inc._and_unions), [its environmental practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_Apple_Inc.), and its corporate ethics, including [anti-competitive tactics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-competitive_practices_of_Apple_Inc.), materials sourcing, and [its acquisitions of smaller businesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mergers_and_acquisitions_by_Apple). Nevertheless, the company has [a large following](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_community) and enjoys [a high level of customer loyalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marketing_of_Apple_Inc.#Brand_loyalty). Apple has consistently been ranked as one of the world's [most valuable brands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most_valuable_brands) since the late 2000s.

## History

Main article: [History of Apple Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Apple_Inc.)

### &#x20;1976–1984: Founding and incorporation

See also: [List of Mac models](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Mac_models) and [Timeline of the Apple II family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Apple_II_family)



In 1976, [Steve Jobs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Jobs) and [Steve Wozniak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Wozniak) co-founded Apple in Jobs' parents' home in [Los Altos, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Altos,_California).



The [Apple I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_I), the company's first product, was sold as an assembled board without a keyboard, power supply, or case.

Apple Computer Company was founded as a [partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partnership) on April 1, 1976, by [Steve Jobs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Jobs), [Steve Wozniak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Wozniak), and [Ronald Wayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Wayne).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer20046–8-5][^cite_note-6] The company's first product was the [Apple I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_I), designed by Wozniak and hand-built.[^cite_note-7] To finance production, Jobs sold his [Volkswagen Bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volkswagen_Type_2) and Wozniak sold his [HP-65](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HP-65) calculator. Although neither received the full selling price, together they raised \$1,300 (equivalent to \$7,400 in 2025).[^cite_note-8]

Wozniak demonstrated the first Apple I prototype at the [Homebrew Computer Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homebrew_Computer_Club) in July 1976, where local computer retailer [Byte Shop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byte_Shop) placed an order for 50 fully assembled computers.[^cite_note-9][^cite_note-Freiberger2000-10] Unlike most contemporary hobbyist computers, which were sold as kits requiring assembly, the Apple I came as a pre-assembled [motherboard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motherboard) with a [CPU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CPU), [RAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAM), and built-in video circuitry, allowing it to connect directly to a household television instead of requiring a costly computer terminal. Users still had to provide a case, power supply, and keyboard. It was introduced at a price of \$666.66 (equivalent to \$3,770 in 2025).[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWozniakSmith2006180-12]

Apple Computer, Inc. was incorporated in [Cupertino, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cupertino,_California),[^cite_note-Apple-10-K-Report-2025-1] on January 3, 1977,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer200410-13][^cite_note-Apple_FAQ-14] without Wayne, who had left and sold his share of the company back to Jobs and Wozniak for \$800 (equivalent to \$4,530 in 2025) twelve days after its founding.[^cite_note-15] Multimillionaire [Mike Markkula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Markkula) provided business expertise and invested \$250,000 (equivalent to \$1,328,000 in 2025) during the company's incorporation.[^cite_note-16] During its first five years, revenue grew rapidly, doubling approximately every four months. Between September 1977 and September 1980, annual sales increased from \$775,000 to 118 million, an average annual growth rate of 533%.[^cite_note-17]

The [Apple II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_II_\(1977_computer\)), also designed by Wozniak, was introduced on April 16, 1977, at the first [West Coast Computer Faire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_Computer_Faire).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer200412-18] Alongside the [Commodore PET 2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commodore_PET_2001) and the [TRS-80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TRS-80), it formed the "[1977 Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Trinity)" of influential personal computers that established the home computer market. Unlike the Apple I, the Apple II was mass-produced as a complete personal computer housed in a plastic case with a built-in keyboard, color graphics, and an [open architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_architecture).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer200412–15-19] Apple introduced the [Disk II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disk_II) 5+1⁄4-inch floppy disk drive in 1978, replacing cassette tape storage with faster and more reliable removable disks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer200413–15-20]



The [Apple II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_II), introduced in 1977, became Apple's first commercially successful computer.

The Apple II became Apple's first major commercial success following the 1979 release of [VisiCalc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VisiCalc), the first widely adopted [spreadsheet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spreadsheet) program and one of the earliest personal computer "[killer applications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killer_application)". Initially available exclusively for the Apple II, VisiCalc transformed the computer into a popular business tool and helped establish Apple in the growing personal computer industry.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer200413–15-20]

On December 12, 1980, Apple became a public company through an [initial public offering](63879.md) (IPO) on the [Nasdaq](21559.md). The company sold 4.6 million shares at \$22 each,[^cite_note-Apple_FAQ2-21] raising more than \$100 million, the largest IPO since [Ford Motor Company](30433662.md)'s 1956 offering.[^cite_note-EDNAAPLSTOCK2-22] By the end of the first day of trading, Apple's [market capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_capitalization) reached \$1.778 billion and more than 300 employees and investors became millionaires.[^cite_note-EDNAAPLSTOCK2-22][^cite_note-DEDIPO2-23]

In November and December 1979, Apple employees, including Jobs and [Jef Raskin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jef_Raskin), visited [Xerox PARC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xerox_PARC) twice as part of a technology exchange negotiated by Jobs, in which [Xerox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xerox) was granted the right to purchase \$1 million of Apple's pre-IPO stock.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsaacson201596–97-24] There they observed the experimental [Xerox Alto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xerox_Alto), which featured a [graphical user interface](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graphical_user_interface) (GUI) navigated with a [mouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_mouse), that inspired Jobs to incorporate a GUI into Apple's future products.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer200474–75-25] Apple's first GUI-based computer, the [Apple Lisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Lisa), was introduced in 1983. Although the Lisa was pioneering as a mass-marketed GUI computer, its high price and limited software library resulted in poor sales.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer200468-26]

Jobs was removed from the Lisa project during its early development and joined the [Macintosh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh) division in 1981. The Macintosh had originally been conceived by Raskin as a low-cost, [portable computer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portable_computer), with early contributions from Wozniak. After Wozniak stepped back following a plane crash, Jobs took over the project,[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer200428,_86–88-28] transforming the Macintosh into a mouse-driven computer with a GUI similar to the Lisa. Wozniak later speculated that Jobs' rivalry with the Lisa project was the driving force behind this shift in direction.[^cite_note-TheVerge-29] Jobs was also hostile toward the Apple II division, which at the time generated most of the company's revenue.[^cite_note-rice19850415-30]

### 1984–1991: Success with Macintosh

See also: [List of Mac models](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Mac_models)



[Steve Jobs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Jobs) in 1984 with the [Macintosh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh_128k), the first successful mass-market personal computer to feature an integral [graphical user interface](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graphical_user_interface) and [mouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_mouse)

In 1984, Apple launched [the original Macintosh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh_128K) on January 24, 1984, introducing it to the public with "[1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_\(advertisement\))", a US\$1.5-million television advertisement directed by [Ridley Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridley_Scott) that aired during the third quarter of [Super Bowl XVIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XVIII) on January 22, 1984.[^cite_note-31] This was hailed as a watershed event for Apple's success[^cite_note-32] and was called a "masterpiece" by [CNN](62028.md)[^cite_note-33] and one of "the greatest TV commercials of all time" by *[TV Guide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_Guide)*.[^cite_note-34] Although initial Macintosh sales were strong, they declined rapidly after the first few months as reviewers criticized its limited 128 KB of RAM, small software library, and relatively high price of \$2,495 (equivalent to \$7,700 in 2025).[^cite_note-35]

Slow Macintosh sales led to a power struggle between Jobs and chief executive officer [John Sculley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Sculley), whom Jobs had recruited from [PepsiCo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PepsiCo) in 1983.[^cite_note-36] The board of directors instructed Sculley to limit Jobs' ability to launch further expensive forays into untested products. Rather than accept Sculley's direction, Jobs attempted to remove him as chief executive.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer2004156–157-37] [Jean-Louis Gassée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Louis_Gass%C3%A9e) informed Sculley of Jobs' efforts to organize a [boardroom coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boardroom_coup), prompting an emergency meeting in which Apple's executive staff sided with Sculley. In May 1985, the board stripped Jobs of all operational duties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer2004156–157-37] Jobs resigned from Apple in September 1985 and founded competitor [NeXT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NeXT), taking several Apple employees with him.[^cite_note-38]

Wozniak had already stepped away from day-to-day operations following a 1981 plane crash. He left active employment at Apple in 1985, citing frustration that the company was prioritizing the Macintosh over the Apple II division, which continued to generate a significant portion of Apple's revenue during the mid-1980s.[^cite_note-39] He remained an Apple shareholder and continued to represent the company in a ceremonial capacity.[^cite_note-rice198504152-40][^cite_note-wozemployee2-41]

After the departures of Jobs and Wozniak in 1985, Sculley shifted Apple's focus toward expanding the Macintosh platform and appointed Gassée to lead Macintosh development, placing him in the role previously held by Jobs. That year, Apple introduced the [LaserWriter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LaserWriter) in March, one of the first affordable [PostScript](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PostScript)-based laser printers, while [Aldus Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aldus_Corporation) introduced [PageMaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PageMaker) in July.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer2004158–159-42] The combination of the Macintosh, LaserWriter, and PageMaker helped establish the [desktop publishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desktop_publishing) market.[^cite_note-43]

In March 1987, Apple introduced the [Macintosh II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh_II), a modular computer with a separate monitor. It was the first Macintosh to support color graphics and feature internal expansion slots, broadening the platform's capabilities beyond the original all-in-one design.[^cite_note-Macworld_2026-44]

Apple's success in desktop publishing allowed the company to pursue a premium pricing strategy,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESwaine2014359–363-45] sometimes referred to as the "high-right policy" because of its position on a price–profitability chart. Under this strategy, Apple focused on higher-[margin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profit_margin) products rather than competing on price, with executives believing that [power users](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_user) would continue to pay for increased performance and capabilities. The policy was championed by Gassée, who promoted a target Macintosh profit margin of 55 percent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarlton199779–80-46] Gassée also opposed proposals to license the [Mac OS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mac_OS) to other manufacturers.[^cite_note-lemms-47]

By 1988, Gassée was viewed by some as a potential successor to Sculley, however, the "high-right policy" he championed began to lose ground as desktop publishing software became available on [IBM PC compatible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IBM_PC_compatible) computers that sold at lower prices. For the first time in the company's history, sales declined in the 1989 holiday season, followed by a 20 percent drop in Apple's stock price.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarlton1997117–129-48] In January 1990, Sculley appointed [Michael Spindler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Spindler) as [chief operating officer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_operating_officer), reducing Gassée's influence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer2004184–185-49] Gassée left Apple at the end of 1990 and founded competitor [Be Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be_Inc.) in 1991, taking several Apple employees with him.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer2004160-50]



[Macintosh LC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh_LC)

The company shifted its product strategy and, in October 1990, introduced three lower-cost models: the [Macintosh Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh_Classic), [LC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh_LC), and [IIsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IIsi), all of which sold well.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer2004128-51] In 1991, Apple introduced the [PowerBook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PowerBook_100_series) line of laptop computers, whose design influenced nearly all future laptops by placing the keyboard behind a built-in [pointing device](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing_device), creating palm rests. The PowerBook was commercially successful, capturing about 40 percent of the notebook computer market during its first year.[^cite_note-Macworld_2026-44] The same year, Apple introduced [System 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/System_7), a major upgrade to the Macintosh operating system that introduced a color user interface and improved networking capabilities. The success of the lower-priced Macintosh models and the PowerBook contributed to increased revenue.[^cite_note-lemsculley-52] *[MacAddict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacAddict)* later described the period from 1989 to 1991 as the Macintosh's "first golden age".[^cite_note-53]

### 1991–1997: Decline and increased competition

However, the success of the lower-cost Macintosh models also cannibalized sales of Apple's high-end systems. In response, the company introduced several new brands, selling largely identical machines at different price points, for different markets: the high-end [Quadra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh_Quadra) series, the mid-range [Centris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh_Centris), and the consumer-oriented [Performa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Performa). This led to widespread consumer confusion.[^cite_note-vawperforma-54] Apple discontinued the Apple II in 1993 after encouraging developers and customers to transition to the Macintosh, bringing the company's longest-running product line to an end.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56]



Newton [MessagePad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MessagePad) with stylus

During the 1990s, Apple also branched out beyond computers, developing several consumer products, among them the [QuickTake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QuickTake) digital cameras, [PowerCD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PowerCD), [Pippin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Pippin) game console, [eWorld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EWorld) online service, [Apple Interactive Television Box](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Interactive_Television_Box), and the [Apple Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Newton) PDA line. Although technologically ambitious, none achieved commercial success.

Meanwhile, Microsoft expanded the reach of [Windows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windows) by focusing on inexpensive personal computers, while Apple continued to emphasize premium hardware with high profit margins.[^cite_note-57] [Apple unsuccessfully sued Microsoft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Computer,_Inc._v._Microsoft_Corp.) over similarities between Windows and the Lisa operating system.[^cite_note-lemms-47] The consumer product flops and the rapid loss of market share to Windows sullied Apple's reputation, and in 1993 Sculley was replaced as CEO by Michael Spindler.[^cite_note-58]



[Power Macintosh 6100](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_Macintosh_6100), Apple's first computer with a [PowerPC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PowerPC) processor, introduced in 1994

In 1994, Apple, [IBM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IBM), and [Motorola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorola) formed the [AIM alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM_alliance) to promote the [PowerPC platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PowerPC_Reference_Platform). That year, Apple introduced the first [Power Macintosh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_Macintosh) models using [PowerPC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PowerPC) processors.[^cite_note-59] In the wake of the alliance, Apple opened up to the idea of allowing Motorola and other companies to build [Macintosh clones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh_clone). Over the next two years, 75 distinct Macintosh clone models were introduced. However, by 1996, Apple executives were worried that the clones were cannibalizing sales of its own high-end computers, where profit margins were highest.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinzmayer2004\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmXnw5tM8QRwCpgPA254_254–256\]-60]

Spindler's tenure was also marked by repeated attempts to modernize the [Macintosh operating system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classic_Mac_OS). The original [System 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/System_1) was not built for [multitasking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_multitasking) (running several applications at once), and although Apple introduced [cooperative multitasking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative_multitasking) in System 5, the company concluded that a more modern approach was needed.[^cite_note-61] Internal efforts, including [Pink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taligent), [A/UX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A/UX), and [Copland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copland_\(operating_system\)), failed to produce a suitable successor, with Copland falling well behind schedule before being abandoned.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-AppleConfidential-63]<sup>: 273–274 </sup>

In 1996, Spindler was replaced as CEO by [Gil Amelio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gil_Amelio), who was hired for his reputation as a corporate rehabilitator. Amelio made big changes, including extensive layoffs and cost-cutting.[^cite_note-64] Amelio began evaluating external operating systems, including [Windows NT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windows_NT) and [Solaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solaris_\(operating_system\)), before entering negotiations to acquire Be Inc. and its [BeOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BeOS) operating system.[^cite_note-65] The talks ultimately collapsed amid disagreements over the company's valuation, with Be seeking substantially more than Apple was willing to pay. Gassée later disputed that the negotiations failed solely over price, stating that disagreements over his prospective role at Apple also contributed to the breakdown.[^cite_note-66]

Apple instead acquired NeXT, the company founded by Steve Jobs, for its NeXTSTEP operating system. Apple purchased NeXT for \$427 million in cash, stock, stock options, and assumed debt.[^cite_note-AppleConfidential-63]<sup>: 277 </sup>[^cite_note-67] At the insistence of Amelio, Jobs accepted 1.5 million Apple shares to lend credibility to the deal and agreed to return to Apple as an advisor.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69]

### 1997–2007: Restructuring and return to profitability

The NeXT acquisition was completed on February 7, 1997.[^cite_note-archive-70][^cite_note-Linzmayer1999-71] Jobs quickly became frustrated with the leadership of Amelio and Apple's board. In June 1997, he anonymously sold his 1.5 million shares, sending the company's stock price lower and contributing to pressure on Amelio, whom the board ousted the following month.[^cite_note-72] Jobs became Apple's *de facto* leader and was formally appointed interim CEO on September 16.[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-Entrepreneur20111027-74]

In August 1997, Jobs secured a \$150 million investment from Microsoft and a commitment to continue developing software for the Mac platform,[^cite_note-75] an agreement widely viewed as beneficial to both companies following [Microsoft's antitrust settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Microsoft_Corp.) with the U.S. Department of Justice.[^cite_note-76] Jobs ended the Macintosh clone licensing program, and in September 1997, acquired the largest clone manufacturer, [Power Computing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_Computing).[^cite_note-77] In November 1997, the [online Apple Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Store_\(online\)) launched and adopted a build-to-order manufacturing model similar to that used by [Dell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dell).[^cite_note-78] By the end of 1997, Jobs had returned Apple to profitability, reporting a profit of \$309 million.[^cite_note-Entrepreneur20111027-74]

The 'four-quadrant' devices



iMac



iBook



Power Macintosh G3



PowerBook G3

Jobs streamlined Apple's product lineup, eliminating roughly 70 percent of existing models and introducing a simplified 'four-quadrant' product strategy consisting of one desktop and one laptop computer each for consumer and professional markets.[^cite_note-79] The restructuring eliminated approximately 3,000 jobs.[^cite_note-Entrepreneur20111027-74]

The first all-new product introduced under this strategy was the [iMac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMac_G3), unveiled on May 6, 1998. Designed by [Jony Ive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jony_Ive), the all-in-one computer featured a distinctive translucent design enclosure, emphasized Internet connectivity (the "i" in iMac), adopted modern technologies such as [USB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USB), and omitted legacy technologies like the [floppy disk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floppy_disk) drive. It sold approximately 800,000 units within its first five months on the market.[^cite_note-800kimacs-80][^cite_note-Alyson_Raletz-81][^cite_note-Time-82][^cite_note-83]

Apple completed the transition to the four-quadrant lineup on July 21, 1999, with the introduction of the consumer-oriented [iBook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IBook) laptop. It joined the iMac and updated [G3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PowerPC_7xx)-powered versions of the professional [Power Macintosh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_Macintosh_G3) desktop and [PowerBook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PowerBook_G3) laptop, completing the simplified product matrix. Jobs said the smaller product lineup allowed Apple to focus more on quality and innovation.[^cite_note-84]

During this period, Apple also focused on building an ecosystem around digital media, similar to its earlier efforts in desktop publishing, through a series of acquisitions and product launches. Apple acquired [Macromedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macromedia)'s Key Grip digital video editing project, which became [Final Cut Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_Cut_Pro) in April 1999.[^cite_note-85] The technology also contributed to the development of [iMovie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMovie), Apple's consumer video-editing application, released in October 1999.[^cite_note-Bell-86] In April 2000, Apple acquired German company [Astarte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astarte), whose DVDirector [DVD authoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DVD_authoring) software was repackaged as [DVD Studio Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DVD_Studio_Pro) for professionals and adapted into [iDVD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IDVD) for consumers.[^cite_note-Bell-86] Later that year, Apple acquired [SoundJam MP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SoundJam_MP) from [Casady & Greene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casady_&_Greene) and relaunched it as [iTunes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITunes), adding a simplified interface and CD-burning capabilities.[^cite_note-87]

In 2001, Apple made three announcements that would shape its future direction. On March 24, Apple released [Mac OS X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mac_OS_X), its next-generation operating system after years of failed attempts to modernize the classic Mac OS. Based on [NeXTSTEP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NeXTSTEP), Mac OS X combined a stable, reliable, and secure Unix foundation with a redesigned GUI. In May, Apple opened its first two [Apple Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Store) retail locations in [McLean, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLean,_Virginia), and [Glendale, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glendale,_California), providing controlled spaces to showcase its products and services.[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-First_stores-89][^cite_note-MacRumors_stores-90] Despite skepticism that it would be able to compete as a retailer, the stores succeeded, and Apple expanded to more than 500 locations worldwide.[^cite_note-fortune-best-retailer-91][^cite_note-storelist-2] On November 10, Apple released the [iPod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPod), its first major consumer product outside the computer market in years. The portable digital audio player became a major success, selling more than 100 million units within six years.[^cite_note-92]

In 2002, Apple acquired [Nothing Real](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nothing_Real) for its digital [compositing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compositing) application [Shake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shake_\(software\)),[^cite_note-93] and [Emagic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emagic), developer of the music production software [Logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic_Pro). The acquisition made Apple the first computer manufacturer to own a music software company and was followed by the development of the consumer-oriented [GarageBand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GarageBand) application.[^cite_note-94] In 2002, Apple also introduced [iPhoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhoto), completing the [iLife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ILife) software suite.[^cite_note-95]



The iTunes Store was highly successful in shaping the legal [music downloading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_download) industry; chart shows the number of songs sold from 2003 to 2010.

In 2003, Apple launched the [iTunes Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITunes_Store), allowing customers to purchase and [download music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Download_music) for 99¢ per song. The service quickly became the leading online music retailer, reaching more than 5 billion downloads by June 2008 and becoming the world's largest music retailer two years later.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97] The iTunes Store helped transform the music industry by shifting consumers from unauthorized file-sharing services such as [Napster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napster) toward paid digital distribution.[^cite_note-Macworld_2026-44]

In January 2005, Apple introduced the [Mac Mini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mac_Mini), a low-cost computer intended to attract Windows users. It was priced starting at US\$499 (equivalent to \$800 in 2025) with users expected to re-use their existing display, keyboard, and mouse.[^cite_note-Macworld_2026-44]



[MacBook Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacBook_Pro), Apple's first laptop with an [Intel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intel) processor, introduced in 2006

At the [Worldwide Developers Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worldwide_Developers_Conference) on June 6, 2005, Jobs announced that Apple would [transition the Mac from PowerPC to Intel processors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mac_transition_to_Intel_processors) over the following two years.[^cite_note-printel-98] The first Intel-based Macs, the [MacBook Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacBook_Pro) and [iMac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMac_\(Intel-based\)), were introduced on January 10, 2006, using Intel's [Core Duo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intel_Core_\(microarchitecture\)#Yonah) processors. Apple completed the transition on August 7, 2006, approximately one year ahead of its announced schedule.[^cite_note-printel-98] During the transition, the Power Mac, iBook, and PowerBook brands were retired and replaced by the [Mac Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mac_Pro), [MacBook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacBook), and MacBook Pro.[^cite_note-99] Apple also introduced [Boot Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boot_Camp_\(software\)) in 2006, allowing users to install Windows on Intel-based Macs alongside Mac OS X.[^cite_note-100]

Between 2003 and 2006, the price of Apple's stock increased more than tenfold, from around \$6 per share ([split-adjusted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split-adjusted)) to over \$80.[^cite_note-101] When Apple surpassed Dell's [market cap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_cap) in January 2006,[^cite_note-modell-102] Jobs sent an email to Apple employees saying [Michael Dell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dell) should eat his words,[^cite_note-103] referencing his 1997 remark that, if he ran Apple, he would "shut it down and give the money back to the shareholders".[^cite_note-104]

### 2007–2011: Success with mobile devices



The newly announced [first-generation iPhone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone_\(1st_generation\)) was on display at the 2007 [MacWorld Expo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacWorld_Expo).

During his keynote at the [Macworld Expo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macworld_Expo) on January 9, 2007, Jobs announced that Apple Computer, Inc. would be renamed Apple Inc. to reflect the company's expanded focus on consumer electronics.[^cite_note-105] He also introduced the [Apple TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_TV_\(device\)) and the [iPhone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone).[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107] The iPhone had been [developed in secret since 2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_iPhone) and was the subject of widespread speculation before its introduction. Jobs presented it as a device that combined the functions of an iPod, a mobile phone, and an Internet communications device. Unlike most contemporary mobile phones, it used a finger-operated [multi-touch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-touch) interface and eliminated most physical buttons. Released in the United States on June 29, 2007, the iPhone sold 270,000 units within 30 hours of release.[^cite_note-108]

In an open letter published on February 6, 2007, Jobs said Apple favored selling music on the [iTunes Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITunes_Store) without [FairPlay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FairPlay), its [digital rights management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_rights_management) (DRM) technology, if record labels agreed to remove DRM from their music catalogs.[^cite_note-109] On April 2, 2007, [EMI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EMI) announced that DRM would be removed from its catalog beginning in May 2007.[^cite_note-110] Other record labels later followed suit, and Apple announced in January 2009 that all songs were available without DRM.[^cite_note-111]

&#x20;In July 2008, Apple launched the [App Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/App_Store_\(iOS\)) for third-party iPhone applications.[^cite_note-112] Within a month, the store had sold 60 million applications and generated average daily revenue of \$1 million. Jobs said the App Store could become a billion-dollar business for Apple.[^cite_note-113]



A docked [iPod Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPod_Classic); Apple worked with other manufacturers to implement purpose-built "[Made for iPod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Made_for_iPod)" docking stations.

On January 14, 2009, Jobs announced that he would take a six-month medical [leave of absence](507071.md) through the end of June to focus on his health.[^cite_note-114] During his absence, Apple reported revenue of \$8.16 billion and profit of \$1.21 billion for its first fiscal quarter of 2009.[^cite_note-115]

Apple introduced the [iPad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPad) on January 27, 2010. It ran the same touch-based operating system as the iPhone and supported existing iPhone applications. Released in the United States on April 3, 2010, the iPad sold more than 300,000 units on its first day and more than 500,000 during its first week.[^cite_note-116] In May 2010, Apple's market capitalization exceeded Microsoft's for the first time since 1989.[^cite_note-117]

On January 17, 2011, Jobs announced in an internal Apple memo that he would take another indefinite medical leave to focus on his health. Chief operating officer [Tim Cook](46878774.md) assumed responsibility for Apple's day-to-day operations while Jobs remained involved in major strategic decisions.[^cite_note-JobsLeave2011-118] In June 2011, Jobs introduced [iCloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICloud), an online storage and synchronization service that replaced [MobileMe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MobileMe).[^cite_note-119] It was the last Apple product launch presented by Jobs before his death.

On August 24, 2011, Jobs resigned as Apple's chief executive officer because of his health.[^cite_note-120] Cook succeeded him as CEO,[^cite_note-121] and Jobs became chairman of Apple, a new position at the company.[^cite_note-122] [Arthur Levinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Levinson) succeeded Jobs as chairman in November 2011 following Jobs' death.[^cite_note-123]

### 2011–2020: Post-Jobs era, new devices

On October 5, 2011, Steve Jobs died.[^cite_note-124] That same month, Apple released the [iPhone 4s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone_4s), which introduced [Siri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siri), an [intelligent software assistant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligent_software_assistant) built into iOS.[^cite_note-125] On August 20, 2012, Apple's rising stock price increased the company's market capitalization to a then-record \$624 billion. This beat the non-inflation-adjusted record for market capitalization previously set by [Microsoft](19001.md) in 1999.[^cite_note-126]

In May 2014, Apple announced it would acquire [Dr. Dre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dr._Dre) and [Jimmy Iovine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Iovine)'s company [Beats Electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beats_Electronics), the producer of the "Beats by Dr. Dre" headphone line and operator of the [Beats Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beats_Music) streaming service, for US\$3 billion. It was the largest acquisition in Apple's history at the time.[^cite_note-127] At WWDC in June 2015, Apple launched [Apple Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Music), a subscription streaming service built on the Beats acquisition.[^cite_note-128]



[First-generation Apple Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Watch_\(1st_generation\)) (2015)

During a press event on September 9, 2014, Apple introduced the [Apple Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Watch), its first [smartwatch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smartwatch).[^cite_note-verge-watchannounce2-129] Apple initially marketed the device as a [fashion accessory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashion_accessory)[^cite_note-wp-fashion2-130] and a companion to the iPhone,[^cite_note-wired.com2-131] before shifting its focus toward health and fitness features to compete with dedicated [activity trackers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Activity_trackers).

In September 2017, Apple released the [iPhone X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone_X), which replaced the physical home button with an edge-to-edge display and introduced [Face ID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Face_ID), a facial-recognition system that replaced [Touch ID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Touch_ID) as the phone's unlock method.[^cite_note-132] The design set the template for subsequent iPhone models. That same year, in June 2017, Apple announced [HomePod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HomePod), its first [smart speaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smart_speaker), aimed at competing with [Sonos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonos), [Google Home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Home_\(smart_speakers\)), and [Amazon Echo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazon_Echo).[^cite_note-133]

Also in 2017, Apple formed a video unit to produce original television series and films, later marketed as [Apple TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_TV_\(streaming_service\)),[^cite_note-134] and in June 2018, Apple signed the [Writers Guild of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writers_Guild_of_America)'s minimum basic agreement.[^cite_note-135]

On August 19, 2020, Apple's share price briefly topped \$467.77, making it the first US company with a market capitalization of US\$2 trillion.[^cite_note-136]

### 2020–2026: Transition to Apple silicon



[MacBook Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacBook_Air_\(Apple_silicon\)), one of Apple's first laptops with an [ARM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ARM_architecture_family)-based processor, introduced in 2020

Apple announced its [transition away from Intel processors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mac_transition_to_Apple_silicon) to [Apple-designed silicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_silicon) at WWDC in 2020.[^cite_note-137] The first Macs powered by the [M1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_M1) chip were introduced later that year,[^cite_note-138] with the company highlighting their improved performance and energy efficiency.[^cite_note-139] The transition to Apple-designed processors also reduced the company's dependence on external suppliers during the global semiconductor shortage.[^cite_note-140]

In March 2022, Apple TV+'s *[CODA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CODA_\(2021_film\))* won the [Academy Award for Best Picture](61702.md), making it the first film distributed by a streaming service to win the award.[^cite_note-141]

In June 2023, Apple announced the [Vision Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vision_Pro), a [mixed-reality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed-reality) headset.[^cite_note-142] It was released in February 2024.[^cite_note-143]

In January 2024, Apple responded to [Digital Markets Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_Markets_Act) regulations by allowing users within the [European Union](9317.md) to use alternative app stores, alternative payment methods within apps, and choose alternative web browsers on devices.[^cite_note-144]

At WWDC 2024, Apple introduced [Apple Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Intelligence), a suite of [artificial intelligence](1164.md) (AI) features integrated across its operating systems.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146]

In 2025, Apple announced plans to invest more than US\$500 billion in the United States over four years, including expanded manufacturing, research, and AI infrastructure.[^cite_note-147] At WWDC 2025, the company introduced the [Liquid Glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_Glass) design language across its operating systems.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149]

On September 1, 2026, Tim Cook stepped down as CEO after 15 years in the role, transitioning to executive chairman of the company's board of directors. [John Ternus](67129733.md) succeeded him as CEO. At the same time, Arthur Levinson, who had served as board chairman for 15 years, became lead independent director.[^cite_note-150]

## Products

See also: [List of Apple products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Apple_products)

Since the company's founding and into the early 2000s, Apple primarily sold computers, marketed under the [Macintosh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macintosh) brand since the mid-1980s. Since the early 2000s, the company has expanded beyond personal computers, beginning with the now-discontinued [iPod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPod) (2001), followed by the [iPhone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone) (2007) and [iPad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPad) (2010). The company also sells products that it categorizes as "Wearables, Home and Accessories", including the [Apple Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Watch), [Apple TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_TV_\(device\)), [AirPods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AirPods), [HomePod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HomePod), and [Apple Vision Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Vision_Pro). Since 2019, Apple has increasingly emphasized its services business.

Commentators have described Apple devices as forming [a cohesive ecosystem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_ecosystem) when used together,[^cite_note-151] while criticizing them for reduced functionality or fewer available features when used with competing devices and for relying on Apple's proprietary features, software, and services—an approach often described as a "[walled garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walled_garden_\(technology\))".[^cite_note-152] [Cory Doctorow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cory_Doctorow), a Canadian [intellectual property](14724.md) researcher and activist, has described Apple's practice of promoting interoperability among its own products while reducing functionality when used with competing devices as an [anti-competitive practice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-competitive_practice).[^cite_note-153]

### Mac

Main article: [Mac (computer)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mac_\(computer\))

A MacBook Air, in midnight color, on a wooden desk.

MacBook Air, consumer ultra-thin notebook computer

An thin iMac in blue color on a desk.

iMac, consumer all-in-one desktop computer

Mac, short for Macintosh, is Apple's line of personal computers that run the company's [macOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacOS) operating system. Personal computers were Apple's original business line, but as of the end of 2025 they account for only about eight percent of the company's revenue.[^cite_note-Apple-10-K-Report-2025-1]

Apple currently produces six Mac families:

- [iMac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMac) – Consumer all-in-one desktop computer, introduced in 1998.

- [Mac Mini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mac_Mini) – Consumer small form factor desktop computer, introduced in 2005.

- [MacBook Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacBook_Pro) – Professional notebook computer, introduced in 2006.

- [MacBook Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacBook_Air) – Consumer ultra-thin notebook computer, introduced in 2008.

- [Mac Studio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mac_Studio) – Professional small form factor desktop computer, introduced in 2022.

- [MacBook Neo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacBook_Neo) – Consumer low-cost notebook computer, introduced in 2026.

Unlike most other personal computer manufacturers, Apple controls nearly every aspect of the Mac platform, developing its own [Apple silicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_silicon) processors, the macOS operating system, and bundled applications including [Safari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safari_\(web_browser\)), [iMovie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMovie), [GarageBand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GarageBand), and the [iWork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IWork) productivity suite. Apple also develops and sells professional applications including [Final Cut Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_Cut_Pro), [Logic Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic_Pro), and [Xcode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xcode).

### iPhone



Backside of an [iPhone 17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone_17)

Main article: [iPhone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone)

The iPhone is Apple's line of [smartphones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smartphone), which run the company's [iOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IOS) operating system. The [first iPhone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone_\(1st_generation\)) was unveiled by Steve Jobs on January 9, 2007. Since then, new [iPhone models](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone_models) have been released every year. When it was introduced, its [multi-touch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-touch) screen was described as "revolutionary" and a "game-changer" for the mobile phone industry. The device has been credited with creating the [app economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/App_economy).

iOS is one of the two major smartphone [platforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computing_platform) in the world, alongside [Android](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Android_\(operating_system\)). The iPhone has generated large profits for the company, and is credited with helping to make Apple one of the world's most valuable publicly traded companies.[^cite_note-154] As of the end of 2025, the iPhone accounts for over half of the company's revenue.[^cite_note-Apple-10-K-Report-2025-1]

### iPad

Main article: [iPad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPad)



The [2020 iPad Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPad_Pro_\(4th_generation\)) on display

The iPad is Apple's line of [tablets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tablet_computer), which run the company's [iPadOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPadOS) operating system. The [first-generation iPad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-generation_iPad) was announced on January 27, 2010. The iPad is mainly marketed for consuming multimedia, creating art, working on documents, videoconferencing, and playing games. The iPad lineup consists of several base iPad models, and the smaller [iPad Mini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPad_Mini), upgraded [iPad Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPad_Air), and high-end [iPad Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPad_Pro). Apple has consistently improved the iPad's performance, with the iPad Pro adopting the same [M-series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_silicon#M-series_SoCs) chips as the Mac, though the iPad continues to receive criticism for its limited OS.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156]

As of September 2020, Apple had sold more than 500 million iPads, though sales peaked in 2013.[^cite_note-500m_ipads-157] The iPad remains the [most popular tablet computer by sales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tablet_computer#By_manufacturer) as of the second quarter of 2020,[^cite_note-158] and accounted for seven percent of the company's revenue as of the end of 2025.[^cite_note-Apple-10-K-Report-2025-1]

### Other products



[AirPods Max](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AirPods_Max)



[Apple Watch Ultra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Watch_Ultra)

Apple makes several other products that it categorizes as "Wearables, Home and Accessories".[^cite_note-159] These products include the [AirPods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_headphones) line of wireless headphones, [Apple TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_TV_\(device\)) digital media players, [Apple Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Watch) smartwatches, [Beats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beats_Electronics) headphones, [HomePod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HomePod) smart speakers, and the [Vision Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vision_Pro) mixed reality headset. As of the end of 2025, this broad line of products comprises about nine percent of the company's revenues.[^cite_note-Apple-10-K-Report-2025-1]

### Services

See also: [List of software by Apple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_software_by_Apple)

Apple also generates revenue from a range of services, including commissions from the [App Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/App_Store_\(Apple\)) on app sales and in-app purchases, extended warranties through [AppleCare+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AppleCare+), cloud storage through [iCloud+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICloud+), and payment services through the [Apple Card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Card) credit card and [Apple Pay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Pay) platform. The company also provides digital content services, including [Apple Books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Books), [Apple Fitness+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Fitness+), [Apple Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Music), [Apple News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_News), [Apple TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_TV_\(streaming_service\)), and the [iTunes Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITunes_Store). [Apple One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_One) bundles several of these services into a single subscription. Since 2019, Apple has placed greater emphasis on expanding its services business as a source of recurring revenue.[^cite_note-AppleVideoRevenue2-160] As of the end of 2025, services comprise about 26% of the company's revenue.[^cite_note-Apple-10-K-Report-2025-1]

## Marketing

Main article: [Marketing of Apple Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marketing_of_Apple_Inc.)

### Branding



The original official logo of Apple was used from 1977 to 1999.

[^cite_note-161]

According to Steve Jobs, the company's name was inspired by his visit to an apple farm while on a [fruitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fruitarian) diet.[^cite_note-162] Apple's first logo, designed by Ron Wayne, depicts [Sir Isaac Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_Isaac_Newton) sitting under an apple tree. It was replaced in 1977 by a rainbow-colored silhouette of an apple with a bite taken out of it, designed by [Rob Janoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rob_Janoff).[^cite_note-web.archive.org-163][^cite_note-164]

On August 27, 1999, Apple dropped the rainbow scheme and began using single-color logos nearly identical in shape to the previous incarnation. An [Aqua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqua_\(user_interface\))-themed version of the logo was used from 1997 until 2003, and a glass-themed version from 2007 until 2013.[^cite_note-165]

[Guy Kawasaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guy_Kawasaki) has described the brand fanaticism as "something that was stumbled upon".[^cite_note-166] [Jony Ive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jony_Ive) said in 2014 that "people have an incredibly personal relationship" with Apple's products.[^cite_note-Time-82]

*[Fortune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_\(magazine\))* named Apple the most admired company in the United States in 2008, and the most admired company in the world from 2008 to 2012.[^cite_note-167] In 2013, Apple surpassed [Coca-Cola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coca-Cola) to become the world's most valuable brand in Interbrand's "Best Global Brands" report.[^cite_note-168] [Boston Consulting Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Consulting_Group) ranked Apple as the world's most innovative company every year from 2005 through 2017.[^cite_note-169] As of February 2023, more than two billion Apple devices were in active use worldwide.[^cite_note-TheVerge20230202-170]

### Advertising

Main article: [Apple Inc. advertising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Inc._advertising)

Apple's first slogan, "[Byte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byte) into an Apple", was coined in the late 1970s.[^cite_note-171] From 1997 to 2002, the slogan "[Think different](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Think_different)" was used in advertising campaigns, and is still closely associated with Apple.[^cite_note-172] Apple also has slogans for specific product lines—for example, "iThink, therefore iMac" was used in 1998 to promote the [iMac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMac),[^cite_note-173] and "Say hello to iPhone" has been used in iPhone advertisements.[^cite_note-174] "Hello" was also used to introduce the original Macintosh, [Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Newton), iMac ("hello (again)"), and iPod.[^cite_note-175]

From the introduction of the Macintosh in 1984, with the [Super Bowl XVIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XVIII) advertisement "[1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_\(advertisement\))" to the more modern [Get a Mac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Get_a_Mac) adverts, Apple has been recognized for its efforts toward effective advertising and marketing for its products. However, claims made by later campaigns [were criticized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Inc._advertising#Criticism),[^cite_note-176] particularly the 2005 Power Mac ads.[^cite_note-177] Apple's product advertisements gained significant attention as a result of their graphics and song choice.[^cite_note-Daily_News-178] Musicians who benefited from an improved profile as a result of their songs being included on Apple advertisements include Canadian singer [Feist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feist_\(singer\)) with the song "[1234](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1234_\(Feist_song\))" and [Yael Naim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yael_Naim) with the song "[New Soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Soul)".[^cite_note-Daily_News-178]

### Stores

Main article: [Apple Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Store)



[Apple Fifth Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Fifth_Avenue) is the flagship store in New York City.



Customers visit the [Genius Bar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genius_Bar) at Apple's [Regent Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regent_Street) store in 2006.

The first Apple Stores were originally opened as two locations in May 2001 by then-[CEO](52234.md) Steve Jobs,[^cite_note-First_stores-89] after years of attempting but failing [store-within-a-store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Store-within-a-store) concepts.[^cite_note-MacRumors_stores-90] Seeing a need for improved retail presentation of the company's products, he began an effort in 1997 to revamp the retail program to improve the company's relationship with consumers, and hired [Ron Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Johnson_\(businessman\)) in 2000.[^cite_note-MacRumors_stores-90] Jobs relaunched Apple's online store in 1997,[^cite_note-179] and opened the first two physical stores in 2001.[^cite_note-First_stores-89] The media initially speculated that Apple would fail,[^cite_note-fortune-best-retailer-91] but they exceeded the sales numbers of competing nearby stores, and within three years reached US\$1 billion in annual sales, becoming the fastest retailer in history to do so.[^cite_note-fortune-best-retailer-91]

Over the years, Apple has expanded the number of retail locations and its geographical coverage, with 499 stores across 22 countries worldwide as of December 2017.[^cite_note-storelist-2] Strong product sales have placed Apple among the top-tier retail stores, with sales over \$16 billion globally in 2011.[^cite_note-retail_army-180] Apple Stores underwent a period of significant redesign, beginning in May 2016. This redesign included physical changes to the Apple Stores, such as open spaces and re-branded rooms, and changes in function to facilitate interaction between consumers and professionals.[^cite_note-181]

Many Apple Stores are located inside shopping malls, but Apple has built several stand-alone "flagship" stores in high-profile locations.[^cite_note-MacRumors_stores-90] It has been granted design [patents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patent) and received architectural awards for its stores' designs and construction, specifically for its use of glass staircases and cubes.[^cite_note-182] The success of Apple Stores have had significant influence over other consumer electronics retailers, which have lost traffic, control and profits due to a perceived higher quality of service and products at Apple Stores.[^cite_note-183] Due to the popularity of the brand, Apple receives a large number of job applications, many of which come from young workers.[^cite_note-retail_army-180] Although Apple Store employees receive above-average pay, are offered money toward education and health care, and receive product discounts,[^cite_note-retail_army-180] there are limited or no paths of career advancement.[^cite_note-retail_army-180]

### Market power

On March 16, 2020, France fined Apple €1.1 billion for colluding with two wholesalers to stifle competition and keep prices high by impeding independent resellers. The arrangement created aligned prices for Apple products such as iPads and personal computers for about half the French retail market. According to the French regulators, the abuses occurred between 2005 and 2017 but were first discovered after a complaint by an independent reseller, eBizcuss, in 2012.[^cite_note-184]

On August 13, 2020, [Epic Games](573199.md), the maker of the popular game *[Fortnite](57813623.md)*, [sued Apple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_Games_v._Apple) and [Google](1092923.md) after *Fortnite* was removed from Apple's and Google's app stores. The lawsuits came after Apple and Google blocked the game after it introduced a direct payment system that bypassed the fees that Apple and Google had imposed.[^cite_note-185] In September 2020, Epic Games founded the [Coalition for App Fairness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_for_App_Fairness) together with thirteen other companies, which aims for better conditions for the inclusion of apps in the app stores.[^cite_note-186] Later, in December 2020, [Facebook](62420226.md) agreed to assist Epic in its legal game against Apple, planning to support the company by providing materials and documents to Epic. Facebook had, however, stated that the company would not participate directly with the lawsuit, although did commit to helping with the discovery of evidence relating to the trial of 2021. In the months prior to their agreement, Facebook had been dealing with feuds against Apple relating to the prices of paid apps and privacy rule changes.[^cite_note-187] Head of ad products for Facebook Dan Levy commented, saying that "this is not really about privacy for them, this is about an attack on personalized ads and the consequences it's going to have on small-business owners", commenting on the full-page ads placed by Facebook in various newspapers in December 2020.[^cite_note-188]

### Privacy



[PRISM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PRISM) is a [clandestine](10864134.md) [surveillance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_surveillance) program under which the [NSA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Agency) collects user data from companies such as Facebook and Apple.

[^cite_note-189]

Apple has publicly taken a pro-privacy stance, actively making privacy-conscious features and settings part of its conferences, promotional campaigns, and public image.[^cite_note-190] With its [iOS 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IOS_8) mobile operating system in 2014, the company started encrypting all contents of [iOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IOS) devices through users' passcodes, making it impossible at the time for the company to provide customer data to law enforcement requests seeking such information.[^cite_note-191] With the popularity rise of cloud storage solutions, Apple began a technique in 2016 to do [deep learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_learning) scans for facial data in photos on the user's local device and encrypting the content before uploading it to Apple's [iCloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICloud) storage system.[^cite_note-192] It also introduced "differential privacy", a way to collect crowdsourced data from many users, while keeping individual users anonymous, in a system that *[Wired](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wired_\(website\))* described as "trying to learn as much as possible about a group while learning as little as possible about any individual in it".[^cite_note-193] Users are explicitly asked if they want to participate, and can actively opt-in or opt-out.[^cite_note-194]

However, Apple has aided law enforcement in criminal investigations by providing iCloud backups of users' devices,[^cite_note-195] and the company's commitment to privacy has been questioned by its efforts to promote [biometric authentication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biometric_authentication) technology in its [iPhone models](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone_models) starting with the [iPhone 5s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone_5s),[^cite_note-196] which do not have the same level of [constitutional](31644.md) privacy as a passcode in the United States.[^cite_note-197]

With Apple's release of an update to [iOS 14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IOS_14), Apple required all developers of iPhone, iPad, and iPod Touch applications to directly ask iPhone users permission to track them. The feature, called "App Tracking Transparency", received heavy criticism from [Facebook](7529378.md), whose primary business model revolves around the tracking of users' data and sharing such data with advertisers so users can see more relevant ads, a technique commonly known as [targeted advertising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Targeted_advertising). After Facebook's measures, including purchasing full-page newspaper advertisements protesting App Tracking Transparency, Apple released the update in early 2021. A study by [Verizon](18619278.md) subsidiary Flurry Analytics reported only 4% of iOS users in the United States and 12% worldwide have opted into tracking.[^cite_note-198]

Prior to the release of [iOS 15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IOS_15), Apple announced new efforts at combating [child sexual abuse material](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_sexual_abuse_material) on iOS and Mac platforms. Parents of minor [iMessage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMessage) users can now be alerted if their child sends or receives nude photographs. Additionally, on-device [hashing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hash_function) would take place on media destined for upload to iCloud, and hashes would be compared to a list of known abusive images provided by law enforcement; if enough matches were found, Apple would be alerted and authorities informed. The new features received praise from law enforcement and victims rights advocates. However, privacy advocates, including the [Electronic Frontier Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_Frontier_Foundation), condemned the new features as invasive and highly prone to abuse by authoritarian governments.[^cite_note-199]

Ireland's [Data Protection Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_Protection_Commission) launched a privacy investigation to examine whether Apple complied with the EU's [GDPR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDPR) law following an investigation into how the company processes personal data with targeted ads on its platform.[^cite_note-200]

In December 2019, security researcher [Brian Krebs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Krebs) discovered that the [iPhone 11 Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone_11_Pro) would still show the arrow indicator – signifying location services are being used – at the top of the screen while the main location services toggle is enabled, despite all individual location services being disabled. Krebs was unable to replicate this behavior on older models and when asking Apple for comment, he was told by Apple that "It is expected behavior that the Location Services icon appears in the status bar when Location Services is enabled. The icon appears for system services that do not have a switch in Settings."[^cite_note-201] Apple later further clarified that this behavior was to ensure compliance with [ultra-wideband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultra-wideband) regulations in specific countries, a technology Apple started implementing in iPhones starting with iPhone 11 Pro, and emphasized that "the management of ultra wideband compliance and its use of location data is done entirely on the device and Apple is not collecting user location data." Will Strafach, an executive at security firm [Guardian Firewall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardian_Firewall), confirmed the lack of evidence that location data was sent off to a remote server. Apple promised to add a new toggle for this feature and in later iOS revisions Apple provided users with the option to tap on the location services indicator in Control Center to see which specific service is using the device's location.[^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203]

According to published reports by [Bloomberg News](3057255.md) on March 30, 2022, Apple turned over data such as phone numbers, physical addresses, and [IP addresses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IP_addresses) to hackers posing as law enforcement officials using forged documents. The law enforcement requests sometimes included [forged signatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forged_signatures) of real or fictional officials. When asked about the allegations, an Apple representative referred the reporter to a section of the company policy for law enforcement guidelines, which stated, "We review every data request for legal sufficiency and use advanced systems and processes to validate law enforcement requests and detect abuse."[^cite_note-204]
